Who we are

IBBAInt is a wholly owned UK subsidiary of ItaG Unibanco Holding S.A. (“Itau Unibanco”) - a leading Brazilian bank with a
market cap of USD 36.9 billion, total! assets of USD 388.1 billion (BRL 2.2 trillion), recurring managerial result of USD 4.8
billion (BRL 26.9 billion) (year-end 2021) and operations in Europe, North America and South America.

Our core business comprises Corporate and Investment Banking (“CIB”) and International Private Banking (“IPB”) to support
the Itad Group’s franchise domestically and internationally.

The CIB operations are concentrated in IBBAInt (London) and in our Lisbon subsidiary, while the (PB business is developed
through our overseas subsidiaries strategically based in the United States and Switzerland.

We have operated in Europe for more than 25 years and are committed to working with and for our clients, developing
long-term relationships with large corporates, financial investors and high net-worth individuals with respect to their
South American cross-border transactions,

IBBAInt's current long term deposit and issuer ratings by Moody’s are A3 (stable outlook), the same as in 2020, reflecting
the Bank’s low level of asset risk, strong capital and leverage metrics and high liquidity.

Itau BBA International
at a glance

We are the Northern Hemisphere banking platform for Itau Unibanco Holding S.A., a leading Brazilian bank with total!
assets of USD 388.1 bn (as of December 2021).

We have operated in the Northern hemisphere region for more than 25 years with a well-established platform, a defined
strategy and a successful business model.

+ We offer a selective range of banking solutions and products to a broad and diversified client base, which has resulted in
steady and sustainable growth.

Our core business comprises Corporate and Investment Banking (°CIB*) and International Private Banking (“IPB”).

Our clients are mainly composed of multinational corporates, global financial and institutional investors with connections
to Latin-America as well as South-American high net worth individuals and regional family offices.

We have a robust risk management framework and conservatively select risks, supported by a well-capitalised balance
sheet and high levels of liquidity.

We operate within a strong regulatory and prudentia! framework supported by a seasoned management team and a highly
skilled Board of Directors including independent non-executives.

We are strategic for the Itau Group, adding substantial value by providing international diversification to our shareholders
and clients and connecting its banking operations to the global international markets and participants.

Foreword

Overview

Itau BBA International pic (“IBBAInt’ or the “Bank’) is the London-based subsidiary of Itad Unibanco Holding S.A. (“Itau
Unibanco’), a leading Brazilian bank with more than 99 thousand employees and operations throughout South America,
North America and Europe. Itad Unibanco and its subsidiaries are collectively referred to as the “Ita Group’.

Our vision and mission

In 2019 we celebrated the 25th anniversary of our banking presence in Europe. Throughout these years, we have operated
in Europe with a clearly defined vision and mission: to be the bank of choice for the most significant Northern Hemisphere
corporates, institutional and financial investors with respect to their South American operations, as well as the preferred
international investment adviser for our South American private banking clients in Europe and in the United States.

During these past years, the Bank has grown steadily to become a truly multinational client centric organisation, aiming
at building and strengthening constructive relationships with all stakeholders, whilst making a positive impact in the
communities of which we are part.

Our strategy

We aim to capture the banking flows between the South American region and developed economies (primarily Europe and the
United States), taking advantage of the itad Group’s leadership positions in Brazil and its growing presence in other South
American countries. °

Our core businesses comprise Corporate and Investment Banking (’C1B”) and International Private Banking (“!P8"); the CIB
operations are concentrated in the Bank and in its Lisbon subsidiary, while the [PB business through overseas subsidiaries
strategically based in the United States, Switzerland, and Portugal (Investment Advisory only). IBBAInt and its subsidiaries are
collectively referred to as the “Group”.

Review of the year

Following an extraordinary challenging 2020, impacted by unprecedented market conditions on a global scale due to the
COVID-19 pandemic, the Bank rebounded strongly in 2021 on the back of a solid economic performance across the globe.
Households responded favourably to the improving epidemiological situation and the gradual relaxation of containment
measures, supporting consumer spending and the overall economy.

Despite the appearance of new and more contagious variants (Delta and Omicron notably) in H2-2021, the high vaccination
rate globally combined with governments’ measures to support economic activity and targeted sanitary measures (travel
restrictions, vaccination passes notably) played a crucial role in containing the crisis, hence mitigating the risks of a durable
recession.

Developed economies which suffered from a brutal GDP contraction in 2020 have recovered faster than anticipated. Despite
mounting headwinds in H2-2021, the rebound of the economic activity within the EU and the USA was sound.

The safety and security of our employees have remained a priority for the Group. The “Work from Home’ approach has been
the norm across our European offices most of this year. In September, once employees’ average vaccination rate reached a
satisfactory level, we implemented a hybrid working model.

In this context, the Bank has once again demonstrated a strong operational resilience thanks to its risk control environment
and state of the art infrastructure. The Bank has continuously invested in new technological solutions as part of its efficiency
plan, while offering the best users’ experience. These investments have enabled the Bank to support employees’ wellbeing
despite the pandemic disruptions, as reflected in the strong results of our annual satisfaction survey.

COVID-19 has had significant impacts on the world economy and the Banking industry overall. The uncertain evolution of
the pandemic fed to adjustments in risk appetite and increased credit provisions. During 2021, with the improvement in the
macro-economic background, we observed a normalization of credit provisions and have in parallel gradually increased our
risk appetite leading to a sustained growth in our credit portfolio in H2-2021.

As of December 2021, the Group’s total operating income reached USD 202.9 m, 5.4% below 2020. Total operating expenses
increased by 8.1% reflecting the investments in new systems and human capital.

° ; Foreword AnnualReport 2021 5

The credit provisions and impairment reversals amounted to USD 9.7 m, positively impacting Group’s net income. This is
a consequence of a more benign macroeconomic scenario, coupled with a combination of asset sales, prepayments and
maturities in our corporate credit portfolio, and highlights our successful risk management strategy in this turbulent period.

Overall, net income reached USD 38.7 m, 21.9% higher than 2020 - a year marked by increased provisioning levels on account
of the new IFRS 9 expected loss approach, an uncertain macro-economic scenario and a conservative credit risk approach by
the Group. ,
In 2021, our consolidated cost-to-income ratio increased to 80.6% and our return on equity was 2.7%. Our capital and liquidity
position remained very strong with a total capital ratio of 22.6%, a liquidity coverage ratio (“LCR”) of 179% and a liquid asset
pool (the unencumbered resources, in amount and quality, immediately available to meet the outflows in an event of liquidity
stress) of USD 2.7 bn as of December 2021.
Our corporate culture
Our values are our identity. This is what sets us apart from other institutions and makes us unique. At Itau BBA International,
our corporate culture known as “Our Way’ drives the way we relate to customers, employees, shareholders, competitors,
suppliers, governments and society at large. It establishes the way we pursue the vision of Itau Group:
“To be the feading bank in sustainable performance and customer satisfaction.”
Among the principles that make up our organisational culture, we value our integrity and ethics, that is, as an organisation,
we take pride in doing what is right, without shortcuts. And we strive to exercise our leadership in a transparent and
accountable manner, fully committed to saciety and the best governance and management practices. For this:

« We always do what is right, even when it is difficult.

+ We would never do anything that could not be published on the front page of newspapers.

At Itau BBA International, “our way’ of making things happen can be summarised in the following key values which are widely
disseminated throughout the Bank and constantly remembered by our employees:

+ It’s only good for us if it’s good for the client;
+ We're passionate about performance;

« People mean everything to us;

* The best argument is the one that matters;

* Simple. Always;

* We think and act like owners;

¢ Ethics are non-negotiable.

Environment, Social Responsibility and Governance

Governance arrangements adequately designed to fulfil the Bank’s regulatory needs in its various jurisdictions while
providing the agility and flexibility to meet our daily goals are essential to our firm.

As part of our continued efforts to improve our governance, we completed two relevant projects in 2021: the Bank's
succession and talent planning and a series of initiatives regarding Diversity, Equity and Inclusion. On the latter, we are
pleased to report that, at the end of 2021, the Bank reached its Board Gender Diversity objective set for 2022 (a minimum of
20% female representation at the Board of Directors) with one quarter of its members being female. We are committed in the
next years, throughout the whole organisation, to further promote female representation and diversity overall. This is a top
priority in our Governance agenda and a recurrent topic of the 8oard annual-self assessment process.

As part of Itau Group strategy, we have continued to more clearly incorporate climate related financial risks into our
governance, overall business strategy and risk management. We have embedded in our processes the importance of
considering climate and transition risks, as well as governance and social dimensions, in the different aspects of our activity.
We have updated our credit selection processes and our procurement strategy, in line with the Itad Group commitments to
reduce 50% of total emissions by 2030 and become carbon neutral (including both direct and indirect emissions) by 2050.

Foreword Annual Report 2021 6

In the selection of our new London office, we were able to procure a space with state of the art environmental setup, which
helped us to significantly reduce our CO2 emissions.

Despite the pandemic we have continued to invest in our social responsibility actions, in line with the Group long-standing
policies in this field. Engagement with the wider community encompassed financial support to philanthropic organisations
and encouraging employees to engage in community service projects, including the support of organisations and individuals
affected by the COVID-19 pandemic as well as voluntary action supporting educational initiatives in the countries where we
are present.

Acknowledgements

commitment in delivering the Bank’s strategic plan and robust operational and financial results. ! am extremely proud of this
year’s achievements and would like once again to thank our employees for their remarkable devotion and adaptability to such
a challenging environment.

Overview

The Bank is a public limited company incorporated in the United Kingdom under the Laws of England and Wales, authorised by
the Prudential Regulation Authority (the “PRA’) and regulated by the Financial Conduct Authority (the “FCA*) and by the PRA.

IBBAInt commenced operations as a UK-based wholesale bank effective 1 February 2013, following a cross-border merger
by absorption (the “Merger”) with Banco Itau: BBA International, S.A. (“ita BBA Portugal”), a Portuguese bank which was
established by the Itau Group in 1994.

The Bank's ultimate parent company, Itau Unibanco, is a publicly listed joint stock company incorporated in Brazil which owns,
inter alia, tad Unibanco S.A. (its commercial banking arm) and Banco ItaU BBA S.A. (its investment banking arm). The Itau
Group’s financial statements are available at www.itau.com.br.

The Bank has subsidiaries in the United States, Switzerland and Portugal-— these al! collectively form the Group, as shown in the
chart below.

As the Bank is a parent company and the directors prepare group accounts, the directors have prepared this strategic report as
a consolidated report relating to the undertakings included in the consolidation.

IBBAInt is based in London with subsidiaries in Lisbon, Miami and Zurich.

On 8 October 2019, as part of the Group’s response to the decision by the UK to depart from the European Union ("Brexit”), the
Bank established a fully owned subsidiary in Portugal — Ita BBA Europe, S.A. (“IBBA Europe’). Following authorisation from the
European Central Bank and registration with Banco de Portugal and the Portuguese Securities Market Authority (Comissado do
Mercado e de Valores Mobilidrios), IBBA Europe started operating as a credit institution and financial intermediary on 3 February
2020. On 18 Octaber 2021, following authorisation from Banco de Portugal and the Comission de Surveillance du Secteur
Financier, IBBAE Europe started operating in Luxembourg through a branch (IBBA Europe — Luxembourg Branch).

Business
Model and Strategy

IBBAInt and its subsidiary in Portugal operate essentially in the corporate and investment banking (“CIB”) segment, where the
strategic focus is on large corporates and cross-border transactions between developed economies (primarily Europe and the
US) and South America. IBBAInt also owns private banking subsidiaries in Miami and Zurich, which are the primary delivery
channels for international private banking (“IPB”) activities for the Itau Group.

Corporate & Investment Banking

The itau Group CIB strategy is based on building long-lasting relationships with clients by fulfilling their banking needs with a
customised products and services offering.

The business mode! for CIB is designed to provide European coverage and execution for CIB clients, with seasoned resources
based in London, Lisbon, Luxembourg, Madrid and Paris, enabling the Itau Group to achieve an effective commercial coverage
for international corporates and financial investors.
The CIB segment focuses on wholesale banking products and services, offered both to South-American corporates undertaking
business in the Northern Hemisphere and large economic groups from Europe, North America and Asia investing into South
America. These products and services include the following:

+ General credit products, guarantees and related products, delivered in a bilateral format or via syndication;

+ Financial derivatives, with an emphasis on hedging instruments for foreign exchange (“FX”) and interest rate risks;

+ Advisory services related to mergers and acquisitions, equity capita! markets and debt capital markets processes primarily
in South America, and potentially in Europe for South American investors;

» Advisory services in relation to project finance in South America;

+ Advisory services, in connection with the broader Itau Group, to support local financing needs for subsidiaries of European
clients doing business in South America;

+ Deposit taking from wholesale clients, mainly large European corporates; and

+ Assistance to European clients seeking to access other products and services (e.g. cash management, FX, etc.) for their
South American subsidiaries.

International Private Banking

The IPB operations are delivered by the Bank’s subsidiaries in the United States and in Switzerland: Banco Itau International
(an Edge Corporation’), Itau International Securities Inc. (an introducing broker dealer), both based in Miami, and Banco
Itau (Suisse) S.A., a bank based in Zurich. These operations are a key component of the Itau Group’s strategy of providing

a global platform of wealth management services to affluent, high net-worth and ultra high net-worth individuals and
families mostly from South America. In December 2021, the Group’s consolidated assets under management were USD 26.5
bn, representing a 13.2% increase versus 2020. This increase underscores the ability of the Bank to expand its business and
attract Net New Assets, which this year reached USD 2.1 bn.

IPB clients have access to unique investment opportunities in the North-American and European financial markets, and to
customised products and services that allow for global diversification of their investments.

The main products offered include:

* Trading of securities;

Structured products;

+ Mutual and hedge funds;

+ Loans;

+ Cash management solutions; and
+ Wealth planning consulting.

Advisory services are provided by experienced teams of relationship managers, supported by investment specialists who
strive to provide the most appropriate solutions according to each client’s risk profile.

The majority of the IPB business is conducted with Brazilian clients, taking advantage of the dominant market share of the itau

client base from the South American countries where the Itau Group has a relevant presence, such as Argentina, Chile, Colombia,
Paraguay and Uruguay.

The IPB activities in Miami and Zurich are complemented with other ancillary activities, such as Treasury. Across the report the
IPB segment figures presented correspond to the performance of these IPB subsidiaries.

Perspectives for 2022

In 2021, the Bank has demonstrated its operational and financial resilience in a relatively unstable environment amid a
pandemic. The Bank’s prudent approach to credit, its robust risk management framework and a disciplined cost approach,
helped it to recover from the previous year, closing 2021 with sound capital and liquidity ratios and a double digit consolidated
net income.

The spread of the Omicron variant in the last quarter of 2021 generated uncertainties on the global economic recovery as its
wave surpassed that of the previous variants in total number of infections. However, thanks to high levels of vaccination and the
overall efficiency of the vaccines, hospitalisations have seen a much more modest increase with lower death rates. The available
studies suggest that the Omicron variant could be less lethal despite being highly transmissibte. Overall, we expect that COVID-
related restrictions and requirements, including in connection with international travel, will have a more limited impact on the
global economic activity, as governments in general, with the exception of China, have been adopting less stringent rules in

this regard. Nevertheless, the outbreak of Omicron in China leads to a downside risk for the activity locally and globally as we
potentially expect collateral effects on the global supply chains during 2022, supporting inflation, Conversely, the rest of Asia
and the Western world have shifted to a more pro-growth stance, appearing as more tolerant to the Omicron surge.

In the USA, we expect Fed interest rate rises in 2022 to control the inflationary pressure. We expect the US GDP growth to
remain strong although slightly weaker than anticipated in mid-2021 due to reduced fisca! stimulus.

In Europe, the impact of the COVID on economic indicators is expected to be mild with improved manufacturing outlook and
GDP growth. Overall, banks are likely to benefit from this uplift in real interest rates and inflationary pressure, Indeed, in this
favourable context, we expect further credit ratings upgrades, and wider credit margins, supporting a gradual increased in

our risk appetite. This favourable outlook on the pandemic front, however, is threatened by the military actions carried out by
Russia in Ukraine, which have significantly increased the geo-political risk in Europe and markets volatility. Likely developments
and their duration, both in terms of Russia’s intentions and future actions as well as of the wider global response, are unclear
at this stage. As a result, it is not possible, at this moment, to estimate with a reasonable level of certainty the potential
consequences of the current conflict on the glabal economy and markets. in the short term, economists are revising up their
global inflation forecast with more immediate concerns over the impact of the unfolding crisis on the price of oil, gas and other
commodities and the consequential changes to FED and ECB monetary policy. The Bank has no direct credit exposure to either
Russia or Ukraine. Similarly, from a funding perspective, the Bank does not have any issuances linked to Russia or Ukraine
credits or indexes. The Bank is currently assessing the indirect economic impact of Russia-related sanctions being imposed and
potential future escalations of the conflict on its business activities.

in Brazil, despite a sharp increase in coronavirus cases after the holiday season, the pandemic situation is contained with a
modest hospitalisation rate thanks to high levels of vaccination. The increase in the SELIC rate (Brazil's reference interest rate)
throughout 2021 placed interest rates at a restrictive level for investments in the country, affecting economic growth. We
anticipate a slower hiking ahead, but no imminent pause in the cycle with an estimated plateau at 12.5% for SELIC (end 2022).
Fiscal stability and sustainability will remain a main concern in Brazil and we expect a contraction of GDP in 2022 mainly caused
by the impact of high interest rates. Inflation is expected to be contained overall in 2022.

The increase in interest rates and, to a certain extent, the increase in Brazil’s country risk premium are likely to impact assets’
valuation translating into lower valuations when measured in hard currencies. This gradual valuation adjustment, coupled with
uncertainties on Brazil’s equity market conditions possibly impacted by lower demand from international institutional investors,
is expected to support the domestic and cross-border M&A activity. 2021 has been a record year in primary and secondary
equities and fixed income issuances with a reasonably moderate M&A activity overall. The ongoing privatization program in
Brazil is expected to also attract substantial interest from locals and international infrastructure-related corporates and funds.

We also expect to observe increased markets volatility in the course of 2022 caused by the uncertainties around the outcome

of the Brazilian presidential election scheduled for October 2022. The local currency (BRL) is expected to remain at historically
weak levels given external and domestic uncertainties. intraday volatility of the BRL (versus hard currencies) might increase over
the year on the back of these uncertainties, which would translate in higher needs in currency hedging overall over 2022 for
international counterparties willing to protect cross-border money flows.

In 2021, the Bank’s revenues were still impacted by the COVID-19 pandemic that led to historic low levels of interest rates, an
adjustment in our risk appetite and, consequently, a reduction in our average credit portfolio. However, with the improvement in
the macro-economic background, we gradually increased our risk appetite, allowing a sustained growth in our credit portfolio in
H2-2021. This growth path on the CIB business is expected to continue if economic conditions normalise and interest rates increase
given that the Bank is uniquely positioned, thanks to its leading positions in cross-border banking solutions, to structure tailored
financial advisory services, hedging solutions and lending. However, notwithstanding the fact that the Bank is not likely to be
directly impacted by the ongoing conflict between Ukraine and Russia, the indirect consequences on the economy are still unclear
at this early stage and so it is premature to estimate the impact of such consequences on the Bank's financial performance in 2022.
In the IPB segment, we had a successful year 2021 and achieved a record Net New Assets ('NNA’) of USD 2.1 bn resulting in Client
Assets & Liabilities (total assets under management, credit portfolio and customer deposits) of USD 31.6 bn. With this new asset
base, revenues will start at a higher level in 2022 and it is expected that NNA will continue growing solidly, slightly below 2021,
because of the uncertainties due to the elections in Brazil and the weak BRL as mentioned above. Increasing interest rates,
primarily in the US, and a strengthening USD have an additional positive impact to the results. IPB has been investing in a major
new IT platform that went five in 2021 in Zurich and early 2022 in Miami. Therefore, costs may remain at the same elevated level
for 2022, but are expected to decrease thereafter. These investments were necessary to enhance the fundamentals of the new
IPB, to better serve its clients and gain efficiencies. The Private Bank also announced a new structure, to adapt to the needs

of our clients, and together with the new system and increasing offering, IPB is best positioned to continuously grow with its
Brazilian market base and to benefit from opportunities within South America.

In 2022, we will maintain a disciplined approach regarding our operating costs while looking for extra efficiencies mainly
through the implementation of new solutions and systems as well as automation processes where possible. We will continue to
expand our Shared Service Centre in Lisbon, a strategy that will contribute not only to optimize our efficiency levels but at the
same time create a centre of excellence that will support all our activities in the Northern Hemisphere.

We will continue to embrace the concept of client centricity, which is the cornerstone of the Itau Group’s strategic agenda.
Commitment to make client satisfaction part of our everyday activities is key for our business sustainability and, as such, the
strategic priority that will continue to guide the Bank in the coming years.

In 2022, our focus will be a continuity of our pluri-annual strategic plan initiated early 2020:

* Look for growth and expansion of our consolidated net operating income, with a prioritization given on fee-related activities,
enhanced by a cross-divisional fertilization approach, We will keep an active but still selective lending activity for large
international accounts, predominantly in Europe;

Deploy a new technology platform for the International Private Banking (IPB), enhancing the client experience, enabling business
growth and scalability, improving efficiency and increasing regulatory and risk capabilities;

Develop new tools to improve our clients’ experiences in digital channels;

Promote efficiencies by challenging the status quo, opting for new technologies selectively, and promote simplification with no
compromise on our controls and risk environment;

Further engage and develop our People, investing in the employee’s journey as a way to continue to attract and retain our talent
and promote diversity; and ,

Further disseminating an ownership attitude across the Bank.

*

We will also continue to build on our strong governance and risk culture, with a conservative approach to credit selection,
maintenance of strong liquidity and capital ratios, as well as our differentiated A3 credit ratings by Moody’s, and focussing

on our clients’ best interest as key foundations of our business model, further contributing to the overall Itau Group’s positive
performance and diversification.

In order to achieve these goals, we believe that promoting high ethical standards, strong governance and quality of
management must remain key imperatives, The Bank is an institution that prides itself on acting with integrity and ethics.
Therefore, we strive to establish lasting and perennial rapports and links with our stakeholders, we value the permanent
satisfaction of our customers through our products and services, we assess the social and environmental impacts of our
financial activity, act transparently and fully in our business and relationships, and adopt practices that contribute to building
shared value. .

As the banking industry undergoes a rapid evolution of its ecosystem and business model, imposing constant adaptation of its
systems and controls, procedures and its offering, we believe it is increasingly important to reaffirm our purpose in order to
promote our values and to continuously improve our governance and processes.

The Bank’s “ESG’ approach, driven by a profound culture of ownership and integrity, is designed around four main pillars:

+ Our People: Developing, engaging and retaining our people responsibly;

+ The Economy: Financing the economy and investing our capital in an ethical way;

+ The Community: Becoming a catalyst of sustainable changes of the society;

* The Environment: Contributing actively to the world’s ongoing initiatives in combatting climate change.

As we address the opportunities and challenges ahead, we will keep our commitment to, and focus on, our long-standing
strategy, aiming to be the financial partner of choice for the most significant Northern Hemisphere corporations and financial
investors with respect to their South American cross-border transactions, as well as the preferred global investment advisor for
our Private Banking clients.

Above all, our confidence is based on the positive record of more than 25 years as the European wholesale and international
private bank platform of the Itau Group, and the full trust and support of our shareholders over all these years.

As of December 2021, the Graup’s total operating income reached USD 202.9 m, 5.4% below 2020, still impacted by the COVID-19
pandemic that led to historic low levels of interest rates and an adjustment in our risk appetite that reduced our average credit
portfolio versus the pre-pandemic levels. Net income on financial operations decreased 68.3%, highly impacted by lower
performance of treasury mark-to-market positions and DVA recognition for the first time in 2020. Total operating expenses
increased by 8.1%, mostly impacted by staff costs growth. This is a reflection of the investment in new systems, which demanded
more human resources for implementation and development. These new systems are intended to support the expected increase
in activity in our business lines. Net profit was USD 38.7 m, 21.9% higher than in 2020, benefiting from credit provisions and
impairment reversals that amounted to USD 9.7 m. This reversal is a consequence of a more benign macroeconomic scenario,
coupled with a combination of asset sales, prepayments and maturities in our corporate credit portfolio. Finally, it is worthwhile
mentioning that our main operational levers showed good evolution, especially in the second half of the year, with our corporate

credit portfolio recovering and closing pretty much in line with the levels of 2020 and our IPB Client Assets & Liabilities reaching a
record level of USD 31.6 bn.

Capital

The Group actively manages and monitors its capital in line with established policies and procedures and in compliance with
local regulatory requirements. On a consolidated basis, the Bank’s regulatory capital requirements are determined by the PRA
under PRA rulebook requirements. The Group’s overall minimum capital requirements under Pillar 1 are calculated by reference
to the regulatory models ("Standardised Approach’ for credit risk; “Mark-to-market Method’ for counterparty credit risk;
“Basic Indicator Approach’ for operational risk; “Maturity-based calculation of general risk” for market risk, and “Standardised
Method’ for credit valuation adjustment risk).

As at 31 December 2021, the Consolidated total capital ratio topped 22.6% (31.12.20: 23.9%) and the Leverage Ratio 14.6%
(31.12.20: 14.4%). These very robust capital levels demonstrate the soundness of the Bank and our commitment to maintaining
the financial health of the Group and to continuously manage the Bank to a prudent strategy and risk appetite.

Risk
Management

Risk is an inherent part of the banking business. The Itau Group considers risk management as an essential instrument to
optimise the use of its resources and to select the best business opportunities to maximise value creation for shareholders over
the long term. The Bank manages risk within the context of the Itau Group-wide risk management framework and the Board
reviews the overall Itau Group strategy to ensure that it is appropriate for IBBAInt.

Our risk culture encourages open and constructive discussion about the risks facing the Bank, emphasising the individual and
collective responsibility of all employees for keeping a long term perspective, with a focus on business sustainability.

The Bank’s risk management framework is designed to ensure appropriate identification, assessment, monitoring and
management of each of the various types of risks underlying its activities on a consolidated basis, thereby allowing sustained
growth of the business.

The Board-approved Risk Appetite Statement includes a balanced mix of both qualitative and quantitative measures covering
reputation, capital, liquidity, earnings, and operational indicators, and is communicated to all employees throughout the Bank,
helping to better align decision-making and risk.

Credit risk refers to the possibility of losses the Bank may incur due to the failure by the borrower, issuer, or counterparty to
fulfil their respective financial obligations under agreed terms.

IBBAInt manages credit risk within the context of the Itau Unibanco Group-wide credit risk management, so that global limits
and guidelines set at the parent company level are also applicable at the IBBAInt level provided that they comply with the
relevant legal and regulatory requirements.

2021 has been a turning year in relation to the progress to integrate climate change into the credit risk framework as a key risk
subject. The assessment of climate risks has become an important topic embedded in the credit analysis, approval processes
and credit risk management processes. Embedding climate risks into a credit risk framework aligned with the business strategy
is fundamental to address, in an appropriate way, the financial risks resulting from physical risks and/or risks from the transition

to alow carbon economy. The impacts of physical and transitions risks can lead to connected risks, which may have the potential

to affect the wholesale portfolio. These impacts must be assessed, monitored, and reported in accordance with the current

governance in place.

For further details on IBBAInt’s approach to climate-related risks, please refer to the ESG section of the Strategic Report.

The effective management of risk involves the following key actions:

+ Analysis and identification of potential risks associated with transactions;

+ Establishment of risk limits;

Strategic Report Annual Report 2021

21

re
+ Definition of policies and procedures that are consistent with established risk limits;
+ Allocation of capital to the risks incurred; and

+ Active management of the portfolio.

Risk Parameters

IBBAInt leverages Itau Group's internal rating methodology, which has been developed taking advantage of the larger group-
wide wholesale portfolio and external databases. This internal rating portfolio is composed of 25 notches, each one with a
specifically assigned probability of default (°PD’). The loss given default (“LGD”) for the Bank’s portfolio is differentiated by
geography.

The general framework involves an expert-led analysis of the legal structure of each country, history of defaults and recovery
rates, and economic scenarios that may impact the corporate restructuring capabilities. Models are independently validated to
ensure the accuracy and continuous calibration of the parameters.

Credit Risk Management Framework

The credit risk management framework consists of the
governance, principles, processes, systems and controls used
for managing credit risk.

A. Risk Appetite

The risk appetite is a framework proposed by the IBBAInt’s
senior management and approved by the Board about the
nature and jevels of acceptable risks undertaken by IBBAInt.
Credit risk is one of the risk dimensions that is included in the
risk appetite metrics. These credit risk metrics are designed
to ensure low NPL levels and adequate diversification of
credit risks. They are frequently monitored and whenever
applicable a defined action plan is presented and approved
by the Board. The risk appetite continued to evolve during
2021, more particularly, enlarging and strengthening our
commitment to monitor socio-environmental risk. We carried
out a strategic review of socio-environmental governance,
integrating the management of this risk into a transverse
and unified model, based on the reputational risk dimension,
according to the fundamentals of the organization’s risk
appetite.

B. Credit Analysis & Risk Rating
The internal risk rating methodology takes into account financial and non-financial variables for each counterparty:

+ Financial statements;

+ Macro Economics;

* Shareholder / Control;

+ Competitive landscape;

« Benchmark (market data and public ratings).
In addition, the IBBAInt rating model includes qualitative and quantitative indicators. The qualitative indicators differ by sector
and take into consideration criteria such as competition, peers, industry trends (growth potential, volatility of demand, threat
of substitution), and diversification (revenue mix, geographica! footprint). Quantitative metrics include financial projections and

analysis of financial data based on cash flow generation, earnings, capital structure, liquidity, financial performance, leverage
and coverage analysis to assess risk rating.

, Strategic Report AnnualReport 2021 22

a
C. Credit approval process
The Bank performs a comprehensive credit analysis (qualitative and quantitative) and all risks are individually approved at the
Credit Committee, based on the company’s assigned credit rating and transaction specifics, such as rating, size, tenor, product,
guarantees and others.
D. Monitor, Control and Review
Limit monitoring is fundamental for credit risk management and is conducted systematically on a regular basis, to ensure that
No approved limit is exceeded without prior authorization. Credit risk is monitored throughout the lifecycle of each transaction
bearing such risk, and includes:

+ Portfolio review, including evolution or average risk rating, client risk rating changes and portfolio duration;

« Control of country and sector restrictions and concentration limits;

* Evolution of the Watch List and Early Warning indicators; and

+ Credit Risk Appetite metrics and Credit key indicators.
Credit limits are controlled on a daily basis and breaches are quickly identified and follow the escalation process defined.
Credit Risk Review is a procedure that aims at ensuring the quality of processes and policies applied throughout the credit
cycle, identifying opportunities and improvements. It comprises all stages of the credit cycle (credit concession, disbursement,
guarantees and monitoring process), as well as processes and policies.
E. Risk Mitigation
Credit Risk can be mitigated by, for example, collateral, netting agreements, derivatives, guarantees and credit insurance.
Additional risk mitigation strategies may be taken in the case of potential credit deterioration above Board-approved risk
appetite including:

+ Mitigation instruments such as Stand By Letters of Credit or Cash Collateral;

« Implementation of strategies to sell the exposure;

« Recommendation to enhance security/qguarantee package;

+ Limit in run off and closely monitoring credit portfolio key risks.
For the IPB segment, credit exposure to clients (‘lombard loans’) is limited by means of appropriate collateral requirements set
by the itau Group, and by controlling and monitoring the market value of this collateral. If the collateral value of the securities
falls below the amount of the credit line, the amount of the loan is reduced or additional securities are requested (‘margin
call’). The Private Banking units apply a haircut to the market value of collateral in order to cover the market risk relating to
marketable liquid securities and to calculate the lending value of each individual collateral.

geography is presented in terms of country of domicile of the credit risk counterparty? and Credit risk by sector is presented in
terms of the industry in which the immediate debtor is engaged.

Market risk

Market risk can be defined as the risk of losses in Bank's on- and off-balance sheet positions arising from adverse movements
in market prices, most typically caused by variations in foreign exchange rates, interest rates, equity prices, index prices and
commodity prices.

The Bank’s Market Risk framework aims to capture all relevant risk dimensions using a comprehensive set of risk metrics, limits
and controls. This framework comprises both aggregate and granular levels of metrics, limits and controls. Historic Simulation
of Value at Risk (one-day holding period and 99% confidence level) and daily control of stress-scenarios are the main metrics on
an aggregate level. The Bank also monitors interest rate and foreign exchange sensitivity as well as market value/notional by
currency at a granular jevel.

The main risk factors are interest rate and foreign exchange rate risk arising from non-trading activity, since the Bank does not
undertake positions with trading intent. The Bank has set in place a granular and comprehensive limit framework, allowing daily
monitoring of the market risks across the Bank. These limits and its utilisation are reported to the senior management on a daily
basis and discussed in the relevant risk committees.

tau Strategic Report AnnualReport 2021 24

Ww

The Bank’s market risk profile reflects a consistent and conservative approach to market risk management in line with the
Board-approved risk appetite.

Further disclosures on market risk are available on Note 41 b) to the Financial Statements.
Liquidity risk

Liquidity risk can be defined as the risk that the Bank, although solvent, does not have sufficient liquid financial resources, in
the short-term and medium-term, to enable it to meet its contractual or contingent obligations as they fall due, or only obtain
them at high cost.

Funding risk can be defined as the risk that the Bank does not have stable and diverse sources of funding, in the medium
and long term, to enable it to meet its financial obligations as they fall due, either at all or without increasing funding costs
unacceptably.

The Group has a comprehensive liquidity risk management framework for managing its liquidity and funding risk in compliance
with Board-approved Risk Appetite and regulatory liquidity requirements. This liquidity risk framework is prudently managed,
primarily through stressed net contractual and contingent outflows under a variety of stress scenarios, measured against the
available liquidity resources (liquidity pool). These scenarios cover a range of idiosyncratic, market-wide and combined stresses.
The Group seeks to maintain its liquidity surplus under these stress scenarios at a prudent but efficient level.

The liquidity coverage ratio ("LCR’) is designed to promote the short-term resilience of the liquidity profile of banks to survive
a significant stress over a period of 30 calendar days. The Group maintains a surplus under the minimum regulatory and internal
requirements, monitoring a range of market and internal early warning indicators on a daily basis for early signs of liquidity risk.

Despite the persisting uncertainty of market conditions resulting from COVID-19 pandemic, the Group was able to maintain

its liquidity pool at comfortable levels in 2021, reaching USD 2,666 m (31.12.20: USD 2,705 m). The liquidity pool represents the
unencumbered resources, in amount and quality, immediately available to meet the outflows in an event of liquidity stress (see
Note 41 c) to the Financial Statements).

The Group continues to have a significant liquidity surplus exceeding the internal risk appetite and the regulatory minimum,
with an LCR of 179% (31.12.20: 153%) and eligible high quality liquid assets ("“HQLA”) of USD 1,904 m (31.12.20: USD 1,617 m),

179%

The Group also monitors its liquidity positions against the net stable funding ratio (“NSFR”) that is designed to promote a
sustainable medium to long-term liquidity profile by ensuring sufficiently stable sources of funding to mitigate future funding
stress. Through 2021, the Group has maintained a surplus above 100%, showing a NSFR of 154% as of 31 December 2021,
according to PRA Rulebook (CRR) Instrument 2021, which has become binding from 1 January 2022.

The Risk, Assets and Liability Committee ("RALCO*) monitors and discusses liquidity positions, respective contrals and
contingency plans through monthly meetings. The Group maintains a Contingency Funding Plan ("CFP’), proportionate to
the complexity, nature, size and business profile, to provide an effective framework to manage a liquidity crisis. The CFP

cca

|

Strategic Report AnnualReport 2021 = 25
complements the Recovery Plan and the Bank’s overall liquidity risk management framework. The CFP provides for actions to
address a short-term or prolonged liquidity stress period. The Bank’s Recovery Plan sets out further credible actions that would
allow the Bank to increase its liquidity resources in the event of severe stress.
Operational risk
Operational Risk is the risk arising from inadequate or failed internal processes, people and systems or from external events. It
also includes other classes of risk, such as fraud, legal risks, physical or environmental risks (e.g. terrorism; natural disasters),
compliance risk and information & cyber security risks. It is inherent to all banking products, processes, systems and activities,
and an effective management of operational risk is a fundamental element of a bank's risk management programme.
The key exercise in 2021 was to implement the Bank wide project “Coverage Ratio for High-Risk Processes”. This activity
sought to determine the type of monitoring in place (indicator, Control, Occurrence and/or Operational! Risk Diagnosis) for the
high impact processes. The outcome provided assurance that we exceed the target defined by the Itau Group regarding the
monitoring of our most critical processes.
For categorisation purposes, the Bank uses the definitions of operational risk adopted by the Basel Committee, as follows:

* Internal Fraud;

+ External Fraud;

+ Employment Practices and Workplace Safety;

+ Clients, Products and Business Practice;

+ Damage to Physical Assets;

+ Business Disruption and System Failures;

* Execution, Delivery and Process Management.
Operational risk control is performed by an independent and specialised function, duly segregated from the business areas.

The operationa! risk management comprises the following steps:

+ Risk Identification — involves a set of actions with the objective of defining internal and external events that may affect
the Bank’s objectives.

+ Risk assessment - measurement, analysis and assessment of impacts and the probability of events / losses that may
be generated, separately and/or on a consolidated basis. This stage involves the development, implementation and
maintenance of a process supported by qualitative and quantitative analyses.

¢ Risk Monitoring and Communication - in the tracking of operational risk events, through risk and performance indicators
and also in the analysis of the control environment through specific evaluation methodologies, walkthroughs, tests and
monitoring of defined action plans.

+ Risk Control and response - development, implementation and maintenance of a process that ensures that the necessary
risk management actions are carried out in a timely and adequate way to respond to the identified risks, namely through
the creation of additional controls in the process, approval of policies and risk appetite procedures and/or metrics.

The operational risk management methodology must be continuously improved in order to:

+ Identify new emerging risks, both internally and externally, as well as review existing ones.

+ Allow for an update of the results of the evaluation process, with the aim of detecting deviations in good time and
supporting the necessary management decisions.

+ Ensuring that adequate controls are in place for the Bank’s activities, products, processes, and systems.

+ Ensuring that the risk management process is informed to management and relevant stakeholders, providing uniform,
timely, reliable, and detailed operational risk information.

cen Strategic Report Annual Report 2021 26

Going Concern

The key risks facing the Group, including those that could threaten its business model, future performance, solvency or
liquidity, as described on pages 20 to 26, are actively monitored and managed by the directors. in particular, based on internal
assessments and three-year forecasts, the Bank’s capita! and liquidity positions are deemed to be robust under both a normal
and a stressed market environment which considers, inter alia, the stress scenarios relating to COVID-19 pandemic. Additionally,
the directors have identified a suite of credible actions to restore the Bank's capital and solvency positions in the event of a
severe stress, which include both support from the parent and market-related recovery options, such as selling corporate loans
to market counterparties.

Having considered these matters, the directors have a reasonable expectation that the Group will be able to continue in
operation and meet its liabilities as they fall due for the foreseeable future.

On behalf of the Board of Directors:

Thomas Campion

Governance

The Bank has a single-tier board structure with dedicated committees focused on audit, risk and capital, and remuneration and
nomination matters. The Board has also established an Executive Committee ("ExCo’) vested with management powers of the
Bank.

The Board exercises oversight of the Bank’s subsidiaries, while respecting the independent legal, regulatory and governance
responsibilities which apply to their respective local boards of directors. Given its regulatory responsibilities for the
consolidated financial health of the Group, the Board is kept informed of and monitors all material risks and issues that might
affect the Group through regular reporting by the top management, including from the risk, compliance and internal audit
functions.

The established governance framework ensures effective communication flow and alignment across and within the Group,
and amongst Itau Group entities. This is largely achieved through cross- and joint-memberships on key governance forums,
including at board level.

The membership of the Board includes experienced directors, fully familiar with the key markets, activities and risks of IBBAInt’s
business.

The Independent Non-Executive Directors ("INEDs’) have wide knowledge of UK governance, and extensive experience in the UK
financial sector, while the Group Non-Executive Directors (“NEDs*) (including the Chairman) hold top executive roles at the Itau
Group level, bringing important shareholders’ perspectives to the Board, as well as deep knowledge of the Ita Group’s overall
culture, strategy and risks.

All members of the Board have a long and extensive background in banking, allowing for independent oversight of risks and
strategic alignment with the wider Itad Group. On average, the Board members have more than 25 years of experience in the
financial sector.

b) Risk and Capital Committee (“RCC”)

The RCC is in charge of providing focused support and advice to the Board on risk and capital adequacy matters. The main
duties of the RCC include:

+ providing advice to the Board on risk appetite, tolerance, capital ratios and strategy, including the oversight of current
risk exposures of the Bank and its future risk strategy, taking into account current and prospective macroeconomic and
financial environment

* providing advice and critical review in relation to the ICAAP and the ILAAP documents;

+ reviewing relevant policy statements and recommending any changes it considers necessary to the Board for approval;

30

ee ie han tikes he Sr REN WT PPR aT sc Ta AAG F SRA TFG 2 RA

advising the Board in respect of overall risk appetite and tolerance, target capital ratios as well as ensuring both qualitative
and quantitative metrics are used to monitor the Bank’s risk management performance;

the oversight and the challenge of the day-to-day risk management and oversight arrangements of the executives;

reviewing whether prices of liabilities and assets offered to clients take fully into account the Bank’s business model and
risk strategy;

assessing remuneration incentives from a risk perspective, including the recommendation of risk weightings to be applied
as appropriate;

monitoring, in conjunction with the Audit Committee, the Bank’s internal control and risk management systems and the
procedures for ensuring the effectiveness of such controls;

reviewing the Bank’s capability to identify and manage new risk types;

escalating to the Audit Committee, as appropriate, periodic updates from the CRO on the Bank’s Recovery Plan and
Resolution Pack; cyber security and information security risks; prioritized risk map; emerging risks, including emerging
risks relating to cyber security, anti-money laundering, business strategy changes, new products, new technologies,
emerging regulatory issues, among others;

«

considering and approving the remit of the risk management function and ensure it has adequate resources and
appropriate access to information to enable it to perform its function effectively and in accordance with the relevant
professional standards; and

providing the advice, oversight and challenge necessary to embed and maintain a supportive risk culture throughout the *
Bank.

The RCC shall comprise at least three members appointed by the Board, with at least two independent non-executive directors
(one of whom shall be the chair of the AC). x

The RCC reviews and monitors actual risk exposures versus approved risk limits on the basis of management information linked
to the various dimensions of the Bank’s risk appetite statement.

The Committee met four times in 2021. The main activities of the RCC throughout the period are listed below:

Reviewed the Bank’s Risk Appetite and risk strategy, introducing or adjusting metrics, including the establishment of anew
metric related to social-environmentat risk;

Monitored the Bank’s risk profile against its Risk Appetite and strategy and reviewed the drivers of changes to the current
profile, such as the COVID-19 pandemic and econamic environment in Brazil;

.

Board;

Closely monitored the Bank’s capital and liquidity positions against the Risk Appetite and targets; considered the results of
stress and scenario testing as set out in the ILAAP and ICAAP;

Reviewed quarterly reports and ad hoc updates as and when necessary from the CRO, including on implementation of key
projects such as Initial Margin, Libor Transition and Climate Risk;

Defined and monitored action plans concerning Single Name concentration risk by rating;

monitored the actions taken by Bank to bring forward the milestones of the related project;

c) Remuneration and Nomination Committee (“R&N”)

The R&N has duties in relation to both remuneration and nomination issues. The chairman of the RCC is also a member of the
R&N, which helps to ensure that there is an appropriate alignment between the incentives (and disincentives) awarded and
prudent risk-taking behaviours.

This committee is required to exercise competent and independent judgment on remuneration policies and practices and the
incentives created for managing risk, capital and liquidity pursuant to its terms of reference. The R&N is responsible for directly
overseeing the remuneration of executive directors, ExCo members and senior officers in the risk management, compliance
and audit functions of the Bank, and of the CEOs and heads of risk and compliance functions of the Bank’s PB subsidiaries. In
discharging its responsibilities, the R&N must take into account the long-term interests of shareholders, investors and other
stakeholders in the Bank. ;

The R&N is also responsible for leading the process for new appointments to !BBAInt’s Board and making recommendations
regarding appointments to the Board taking into account the challenges and opportunities facing the Bank, and what skills and
expertise are therefore needed on the Board.

The R&N shall comprise at least three members appointed by the Board amongst the non-executive directors, with at least two
independent non-executive directors.

The R&N met three times in 2021. The main activities of the R&N over this period are listed below:

.

Considered and approved compensation (including bonus awards and role-based allowances as applicable) for the Bank’s
Executive Committee members, Heads of Internal Audit and Compliance, as well as for the Bank’s subsidiaries CEOs and
CROs; . ,

Considered and recommended appointment of Board members;

Received updates on CRD V and its transposition into the UK, including with respect to the new proportionality criteria;

*

employees therefrom;

Reviewed and recommended the Remuneration Policy for Board approval;

*

heads of control functions of the Bank; , ‘

remuneration payable to the material risk takers of the Bank;

+ Recommended for Board approval the R&N Terms of Reference;

Executive Committee (“ExCo”)

The Board has established the ExCo, led by the CEO, to which the Board delegates general management powers allowing the
ExCo to manage the Bank’s business on a day-to-day basis.

The appointment of the ExCo members is a matter reserved to the Board. In appointing ExCo members, the Board is expected to
ensure that there is an appropriate mix and balance of skills, experience, and knowledge to enable the ExCo to discharge their
management responsibilities effectively, with regard to the key activities and risks in the Bank’s business model.

Under the authority of the Board, the ExCo must manage the business in line with the Board-approved strategy and risk
appetite. .

Through the approval of risk policies and procedures and the establishment of governance structures (e.g. management
committees), the ExCo ensures that the Board-approved risk appetite is translated into risk limits and embedded into the
strategic, financial planning, and overall decision-making processes.

The ExCo is accountable to the Board and must report and escalate matters of particular significance (even matters within the
delegated mandate).

Strategic Report Annual Report 2021

33

Statement

as to Section 172(1)
of the Companies
Act 2006

Section 172(1) of the Companies Act 2006 requires directors to act in the way that they consider, in good faith, would be most
likely to promote the success of the Bank for the benefit of its shareholders as a whole. In doing so, directors are required

to have regard to a non-exhaustive list of factors, including the interests of the wider stakeholders of the Bank, such as the
interests of employees and how the decisions made affect customers, suppliers, the community and the environment, as well as
the Bank’s reputation. The Companies (Miscellaneous Reporting) Regulations 2018 require directors to explain in the strategic
report how they considered the interests of key stakeholders and the broader matters set out in section 172(1) (a) to (f) when
performing their duty to promote the success of the Bank under section 172 (°S172").

The stakeholders we consider in discharging our duties under $172 are the people who have business relations with us, work

for us, regulate us, invest in us and live in the communities we serve. The matters we consider most when taking decisions are
the ones set out in section $172, i.e. the likely consequences of these decisions in the long term; the interests of the Bank’s
employees; the need to foster the Bank’s business relationships with suppliers, customers and others; the impact of the Bank’s
operations on the community and the environment; and the ability of the company maintaining a reputation for high standards
of business conduct; and the need to act fairly. To comply with these requirements, the Bank invests in well prepared and
organised Board processes, as they are instrumental to ensuring good governance and well-grounded decision making at Board
level.

During 2021, one area of special attention was the impact of the ongoing COVID-19 pandemic on our stakeholders which
ultimately affected the Bank’s strategy. The Board was kept abreast, inter alia, on how the pandemic was impacting the Group’s
employees’ mental and physica! health, on the “work from home’ and “return to the office’ strategies, and on the effect of the
evolution of the pandemic on our customers.

Another area that required special focus from the Board in 2021 was the implementation of the Group set up following the UK's
withdrawal from the European Union (EU) on 31 January 2020 and the end of the transition period on 31 December 2020 (the
so-called “IP completion day”).

In preparation for meaningful discussions that allow consideration of the factors set out in $172 in the decision making process,
the directors are provided with the Board papers in advance, having time to weigh up the decisions they consider most likely

to promote the success of the Bank. Board papers are prepared and reviewed by the ExCo with the aim of providing the Board
with all the information the directors need to make a well-grounded judgement as to the best course of action in the light of
the Bank’s strategic objectives in the long term which considers the outcome of principal decisions for the various stakeholders.
The information sent to the directors in advance is supplemented through in-person presentations from across the senior
management team. For a full description of the Bank’s Board processes and wider governance, including the role of the Board
committees and the work performed during 2021 in supporting the full Board decision making, please refer to the previous
section on Governance (pages28 to 33).

Decision making

We set out below examples of how the directors have considered the implications of their principal decisions in the long term
and with regard to the impact of such decisions on the Bank’s key stakeholders.

in March 2021, the Board was asked to consider to approve a capital line of up to EUR 100 million in favour of the Bank’s new
banking subsidiary in Portugal, Itau BBA Europe, S.A. (“IBBAE”), for capital increases to support the EU-based activity to be
carried out through this EU-licensed entity following the IP completion day. In doing so, the directors have had regard to the
matters set out in $172 and the outcome of the decisions made for all the relevant stakeholders.

The directors considered that strengthening the equity of the EU subsidiary would be in the best interest of the Bank in the
long run as it is instrumental in delivering a key component of its strategy, i.e. providing banking and investment services to the
most significant EU-based corporations and financial investors with respect to their South American cross-border transactions.
The directors specifically considered the importance of having the right level of capital at IBBAE in order to better serve its EU
clients after the end of the transition period. The directors also considered that, rather than implementing an upfront capital
increase of EUR 100 million in IBBAE, a phased implementation, in consonance with the pace of growth of IBBAE’s EU business,
would offer the best guarantee of prudent and sound capital management across the Group, which would be aligned with the
interests of the shareholders, creditors, suppliers, employees and regulators of the Bank.

In September 2021, the Board was required to consider and make a decision regarding the transfer, by way of an interim
dividend in kind (“Dividend”), of the Bank’s shareholding in Itau Europa Luxembourg, S.A. (‘IE’) to its sole shareholder ITB
Holding Brasil Participagdes Ltda. (the “Shareholder’). At that point in time, IEL had already transferred the entire issued share
capital of its subsidiary Banco Itau Suisse S.A. to the Bank. After carefully considering all the legal requirements and having
satisfied themselves that (a) the payment of the Dividend would not affect the Bank’s ability to pay its foreseeable debts as
they fell due and (b) the amount of the Bank’s net assets was not less than the aggregate of its called up share capital and
undistributable reserves and (c) the distribution would not reduce the amount of the net assets to less than that aggregate,
the directors specifically considered their duty to promote the success of the Company and the other matters referred to in
$172 of the CA 2006. In particular, the directors considered the fact that, with the payment of the Dividend, the Bank would be
endorsing the simplification of the Group’s corporate structure and enabling the removal of a non-operative company from

its consolidation perimeter. This outcome was considered by the directors as being in tine with the expectations of the Bank’s
regulators. The directors also specifically considered the fact that the payment of the Dividend would not be detrimental to the
employees of the Bank, nor would have any negative impact on the Bank’s relationships, reputation and operations. As such,
when discharging their duties, the directors have had regard to the matters set out $172 and the outcome of the decisions made
for all the relevant stakeholders.

Within the reflections weighed by the directors in deciding how to act, and consistent with the Bank’s recognition of the
importance to have a lean and efficient corporate structure, the decision to transfer the entire issued share capital of IEL to the
Shareholder was considered by the directors as the most likely to promote the success of the Bank.

In November 2021, upon recommendation from its Risk and Capital Committee ("RCC’), the directors approved the introduction
in the Bank’s risk appetite framework of a new metric related to social-environmental risk, whereby credit exposure
concentration thresholds for high risk sectors were defined and became part of the regular CRO reports to the RCC. In 2021, the
focus on the continuing sustainability of the Bank’s business madel has led to increased time devoted by the RCC to social and
environmental factors affecting the Bank's strategy.

For further information on the methods used by the Board to deliver on its commitment to effectively engage with all of its
stakeholders, please refer to page 48 — “Statement as to engagement with suppliers, customers and others in a business
relationship with the Bank”.

ESG

Environmental, Social, and Corporate Governance (‘ESG’) refers to the three central factors in measuring the sustainability and
sacietal impact of companies and businesses. Stakeholder focus on these wider ESG factors has been evolving in recent years.

Investors, customers and clients, regulators, policy makers and broader society are all accelerating the pace of change.

The Itau Group has been a leader in ESG implementation in South America, incorporating sustainability into its corporate

strategy through a governance structure that is consolidated and integrated within its business, namely through the ‘Positive

impact commitments’, a set of sustainability commitments that resulted from the review of its strategic guidelines.

As part of the Itai Group, IBBAInt is also working on strengthening the integration of social and environmental issues into its

business strategy, aiming to become a more responsible, sustainable and inclusive Bank.

In defining its goals and delivering its strategy, IBBAInt aims to act sustainably and responsibly and in line with its corporate
culture.

In this scope, IBBAInt believes that promoting high ethical standards, good governance and quality of management are key
imperatives, The Bank has been seeking to promote Corporate Sacial Responsibility, Diversity & Inclusion and Risk Culture
programmes, and also enhancing its risk management framework in respect to climate change financial risks, leveraging on

the Itad Group’s experience in this area to effectively consider our portfolios, asset allocation and lending policies through an

additional climate risk lens.

Corporate Social Responsibility

The “Itau Social Program’, managed by Fundacdo Itau Social (Sociat Ita Foundation), aims to coordinate the Ita Group’s role
in supporting or developing social, scientific and cultural projects, mainly in the areas of education, health, culture, sport and

mobility.

At the start of the current pandemic, in April 2020, Itad Group showed once again its leadership in Corporate Social

Responsibility by created the initiative “Todos pela Saude’ (All for Health) from the donation of BRL 1 billion, with the purpose

of combating the new Coronavirus and its effects on Brazilian society. This was the highest private donation ever made by a

taupe Strategic Report Annual Report 2021

36

" : Strategic Report Annual Report 2021 37
cen gic Rep p

Brazilian corporate and was further increased by the bank’s shareholders to a total amount of BRL 1.25 billion. “Todos Pela
Satide” was converted into an institute in February 2021, to be supported by “Fundagdo Itau’, with the mission of strengthening
and innovating in the sanitary surveillance field in Brazil.

At IBBAInt, engagement with the wider community is mainly developed through the Bank’s social responsibility initiatives. These
initiatives range from providing financial support to philanthropic organisations, such as Save the Children, to encouraging
employees to engage in community service projects, including the support of organisations and individuals affected by the
Covid-19 pandemic. In 2021, the Bank also took part in the Global Voluntary Action campaign led by Itau Social in Brazil (the
“Read with a Child” initiative), which supports educational initiatives in the countries in which the Itad Group is present.

Customer satisfaction

As part of the Itau Group, the Bank believes that client satisfaction is key to delivering its strategy and operating its business in
a way that is sustainable in the long term. tau Group aims to be a benchmark in customer satisfaction, seeking inspiration from
companies that are leaders in this subject, regardless of their business sector.

IBBAInt’s business model is focussed on offering specialised services and tailor-made products to a niche segment, consisting of
wholesale clients and high net worth clients with connections to the South America market. This niche-banking model implies a very
close relationship with a selected client base. The Group’s wholesale and private banking management models are based on direct
and personal interaction with our clients, obtaining an in-depth understanding of their needs and offering customised solutions.

Keeping the client at the centre of our decision-making process requires the Bank to continuously improve its initiatives and
processes. To reinforce this commitment and to ensure that customer satisfaction is recognised and rewarded, the Bank
reviewed its performance evaluation criteria to include client centricity as one of the key pillars of the employees’ assessment,
and daily disseminates a culture where the customer continues to be at the centre of our operations.

Captivating the customer, fostering a clear, simple, and transparent communication with the client and taking the client’s
problem as their own problem, are seen as everyone’s responsibility at the Bank.

Employees - Diversity & Inclusion

The Itau Group has been taking several steps to transform and improve the employees’ experience through their careers within
the Group. As part of the ItaG Group, the Bank shares the vision that people should be valued as they are, their experiences,
characteristics and mindsets, and that barriers should be eliminated so that all employees can develop their potential in an
empowering workplace.

Throughout the years, the Bank has continued to further strengthen its Diversity & Inclusion initiatives, The Bank maintains that
by creating a diverse and inclusive environment, which is about recognising the potential all employees bring to the workplace
and of the right of every employee to be treated with respect and dignity throughout their employment with the Bank and not
to be discriminated against. To achieve this goal, the tau Group created a Global Diversity Programme, aimed at educating,
informing and fostering a connection with others, whilst also creating a working environment which allows all individuals to be
treated fairly, equally and respectfully.

Locally, IBBAInt started a Diversity & Inclusion journey based on a strategy with 6 different pillars (working with communities,
wellbeing, gender, race, LGBTI+ and disabilities). During 2021 and encompassed in this strategy, a new series of webinars called
the ‘Courageous Talks’ were launched with special focus on race. Each webinar has a key note speaker who shares his experience
and insights on a particular topic within the theme of Diversity & Inclusion with the intention of giving visibility, creating
awareness and stimulating engagement around this central topic.

In addition to the above, IBBAint launched a campaign to raise awareness on minorities’ rights by celebrating key global dates
that are a reference for certain marginalised groups. This allows a continued engagement with the history of these groups’ fight
for freedom and equality. IBBAInt believes that educating ourselves about the plight of minorities and the factors that influence
discrimination is the first step to changing the narrative around subordinate groups.

To support the Diversity & Inclusion strategy, development and retention of our employees, the Bank has also expanded the
learning programme for all employees under the 1OX Academy far Europe, with specific learning paths where employees can find
and explore various digital content on unconscious bias, inclusion in the workplace, courageous conversations, gender, race and
working with people with disabilities.

In the wake of the COVID-19 pandemic, the Bank has invested in applications, infrastructure and equipment to allow all our
employees to work remotely while looking to keep the collaborative spirit that characterizes our culture. More recently,
investments have been made to allow the return to the office in a safe manner.

|

As part of our return-to-work strategy, in 2021 the Bank launched a new “Smart Working’ programme (3:2 work pattern
implemented since September 2021), which creates a hybrid way of working at home and at the office which we believe will
improve our working culture and efficiency by allowing for more flexibility for our teams. This hybrid model is supported

by a digital platform (work in office), where all employees can book in advance their days at the office. Moreover, we have
implemented a corporate wellness program that contains a range of content related to working in a crossbreed environment,
mental and physical health and leading remote teams.

In addition to the above, the Bank also continued its Employee Experience Programme, this is another longer-term programme
which was designed to improve the full employee experience; mapping the employee journey, identifying areas of improvement
and change and actioning these.

IBBAInt continues to take part in the Itau Group climate surveys to measure employee engagement and analyse results
thoroughly. Any people initiatives are based on results of the survey. In addition to the Board receiving and considering updates
on the results of these surveys from senior management in relation to employees, members of the Board engage with the
Bank’s employees, holding ad hoc meetings with technical areas and meeting with employees both in the UK and abroad. Town
Halls take place regularly to communicate the Bank’s performance against strategic goals, share company news and employee
initiatives and create another opportunity for direct dialog and exchange between the staff and the top managers of the Bank,
including members of the Board. ;

Suppliers

The Bank is also committed to maintaining a constructive and ethical relationship with its suppliers. When considering

a supplier in a procurement process, the Bank considers factors such as expertise, reputation, risks, terms & conditions,
relationship and potential conflicts of interest. The procurement of goods and services must follow the provisions set forth by
the Procurement Policy pursuant to which the Bank will only work with reputable, lawful, ethical, socially and environmentally
responsible suppliers. The selection of any supplier follows a due diligence process consisting of an analysis of their adherence
to compliance standards and to the applicable legislation, such as Anti-Bribery, Anti-Corruption and Anti-Slavery regutations,
European Banking Authority guidelines and PRA Supervisory Statements on outsourcing arrangements, as well as to social and
environmental responsibility, diversity and inclusion practices.

Environment

In line with the efforts to tackle global climate emergency, both international and local initiatives have gained momentum
and pushed the private sector to act. As signatories to the Principles for Responsible Banking‘, Itau Unibanco is committed
to aligning its efforts with the Paris Agreement, which is aimed at limiting global warming average temperature to below 1.5
degrees Celsius. To do so, the world must halve carbon emissions by 2030 (2010 base year) and become Net zero by 2050.

As announced in October 2021, the itau Unibanco’s strategy, which is already carbon neutral5, for scopes 1 and 2 (own operation
and energy) is to reduce emissions from its loans by 50% by 2030 and become carbon neutral in the loan portfolio by 2050. To
accomplish this, besides announcing in July 2021 a BRL 400 billion commitment by 2025 to promote a sustainable, increasingly
greener and more inclusive economy, it has set up the following action plans:

Support clients along their transition journey - supporting the development of and fostering a climate transition plan for
